_source line 7095 · 2026-05-23T10:00:50.803Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating final revisions of the OntoCoDec EMNLP 2026 paper (DDL May 25, 2026). This session handled:
   
   **R23 Phase 4 completion (NF2, NF6, NF7)**: Finished remaining Nice-to-Have items from R23 major restructuring — reducing "enabling dependency" term frequency, formalizing first-seen registration in §3.3, and compressing Limitations section by ~37%.
   
   **R24 full patch**: Director issued comprehensive R24 with 3 critical MF, 8 SF warnings, and minor items. All executed except MF1 DFA bounds (waiting for Agent data on actual entity counts).
   
   **R24 Patch Supplement**: Director sent 3 corrections: (1) SF8 beam width — use actual k=2 sweep data instead of speculative discussion, (2) MF1 DFA — wait for Agent median entity count data, (3) MF3 70B — "single-family scale validation" instead of "preliminary". Plus 2 new references (KG-Trie, Vukovic).

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - OntoCoDec: Two-layer constrained decoding (grammar CFG + semantic context-sensitive) for IE
   - "Empirical enabling dependency" — scale-dependent interaction between grammar and semantic layers (term frequency reduced to 2 instances in main text)
   - First-seen registration semantics: entity type committed at first occurrence governs all subsequent checks
   - DR↔CF trade-off: per-triple independent enforcement architectural limitation
   - Beam sweep data (d066_beam_sweep): k=2 is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11%, 1.18× latency)

        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, SGLang~\\cite{zheng2024sglang}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking but lack cross-field semantic awareness.\nPICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata; KG-Trie~\\cite{luo2025kgtrie} applies trie-based KG constraints during decoding for knowledge-to-text generation.\nOntoCoDec applies constructive forward masking via a dynamic entity registry, enforcing schema semantics within a single forward pass.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, and others~\\cite{guidex2025,josifoski2022genie,paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,wang2023instructuie,qi2024adelie,fei2022lasuie,lu2022uie,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure via training-time internalization without runtime enforcement~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE-7B achieves zero DR under zero-shot evaluation, but at the cost of near-complete output suppression (5 predicted relations vs.\\ ${>}$1{,}100 for OntoCoDec under LoRA, 46\\% empty output; Appendix~\\ref{app:gollie-comparison}); direct comparison is methodologically constrained by incompatible evaluation protocols.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\\% of predictions.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations; \\citet{vukovic2024dialogue} combines ontology constraints with constrained chain-of-thought for dialogue RE---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step without iterative refinement.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}).\n",

        "totalLines": 19
      }

        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning near-eliminates domain/range violations ($<$\\,0.1\\%) at F1 41--47\\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\\leq$1.4\\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nBeam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\\times$.\nIn zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\\%) but decrease with model calibration.\nZero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 14,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 14
      }
    },

        "content": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR elimination ($\\leq$0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n    \\item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 31,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 31
      }

        "content": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\n% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep\nWith LoRA, beam search~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED at both 8B and 70B scales (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nA beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\\%, at only 1.18$\\times$ greedy latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\\times$ latency (Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\nThis yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\\%, CF${\\leq}$1.4\\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\\times$, all violations${\\to}$0\\%).\nPer-beam memory overhead is $O(k \\times |\\text{entities}| \\times |\\text{types}|)$ for independent entity registry copies, negligible at typical document scales.\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B-class validation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nThe following results constitute a single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4); cross-family verification (e.g., Qwen-72B) remains future work.\nAll 70B experiments use Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct under AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms constraint enforcement is unaffected by quantization (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\nZero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; R-F1 11--13).\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)\nA four-way ablation reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\\% DR reduction vs.\\ 8B's 24\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), though grammar remains necessary for RV elimination and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained\nLoRA confirms composability: DR\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\\Delta| \\leq 0.20$\\,pp; CF 0.18--1.33\\%); cross-family generalization at 70B scale remains to be verified.\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_docred_200_beam4_s1\nBeam ($k{=}4$) reduces DocRED parse failures (12.5\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.5\\%, R-F1 $+$4.19\\,pp); SciERC controlled comparison isolates pure beam gain at $+$0.28\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence on Qwen3-8B (DR${=}$15.26\\%; other models use the same backend; Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nThe ${\\sim}$15\\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\\% total reduction); trie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\\% parse failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}).\n",
        "numLines": 34,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 34
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section5_runtime.tex"
  },

{
  "parentUuid": "3202f0e5-8e7c-40cb-9e51-e0834fb4fe27",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex",
        "content": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nBecause IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.\nThis scale-dependent interaction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp).\nThis is an architectural trade-off of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, first-seen type registration (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.\nBeam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at only 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nThe trade-off represents a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance.\nOntoCoDec provides a runtime guarantee layer---the two approaches are complementary rather than competing.\nLoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\\%, and constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nThe fundamental tension is that per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations, which correspond to Level~3 (non-local) constraints in our taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}).\nGlobal enforcement requires maintaining entity-level type consensus across the full output sequence, exceeding what online per-token masking can achieve.\nBeam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.\nConstraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.\n",
        "numLines": 23,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 23
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex"

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. Returns step-by-step plans, identifies critical files, and considers architectural trade-offs. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- statusline-setup: Use this agent to configure the user's Claude Code status line setting. (Tools: Read, Edit)"

201	Greedy constrained (S1)& 2.873 & 1.10$\times$ \\
202	Beam-4 unconstrained   & 3.446 & 1.32$\times$ \\
203	Beam-4 constrained (S1)& 4.013 & 1.54$\times$ \\

206	\caption{Latency per document (SciERC, 551 docs, Qwen3-8B LoRA FT, RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell). Overhead relative to greedy unconstrained.}
207	\label{tab:beam-latency}
208	\end{table}
209	
210	Beam search itself adds 32\% latency (4 beams); constraint overhead increases from 10\% (greedy) to 17\% (beam) because each beam independently maintains grammar and semantic state.
211	Per-beam entity registry memory is $O(|\text{entities}|)$ per beam; at $k{=}8$ with typical document sizes, overhead is ${<}$1\,MB per beam, negligible relative to model memory.
212	The total 1.54$\times$ overhead is acceptable for offline batch IE; \texttt{early\_stopping} limits additional compute to partial-sequence exploration.
213	
214	We also explored a type-consensus constraint that forces identical entity types across all relations within a document.
215	However, CF violations occur precisely where the consensus type conflicts with domain-range constraints, creating a fundamental CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off.
216	With DR-aware enforcement (skipping consensus when it would violate domain-range), the layer becomes a no-op (100\% skip rate on 551 SciERC documents and 50 DocRED documents).
217	This confirms that beam search resolves the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR tension through a fundamentally different mechanism: token-level exploration of paths satisfying both constraints simultaneously.
218	
219	\paragraph{TCR reranking under beam search.}

    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 41--47\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with a scale-dependent layer interaction: at 8B, the semantic layer achieves only 24\\% independent DR reduction (near-complete when combined), while at 70B it reaches 84\\% independently.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 41--47\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on both RE datasets under fine-tuning (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with total violations decreasing $\\sim$31\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\n\nOntoCoDec is a constraint enforcement method, orthogonal and complementary to training-based schema-aware approaches such as GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} and KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}; it can be composed with any generative IE system to improve schema compliance without retraining.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework, formalizing a known intuition for the IE setting: that domain/range type constraints require stateful enforcement beyond context-free grammars (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item Systematic ablation across 3 model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) and 2 datasets (SciERC, DocRED) under both zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned settings, demonstrating DR elimination ($\\leq$0.09\\%) with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; DR enforcement introduces CF as a correctable side effect (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); a preliminary $n{=}20$ event extraction pilot provides directional evidence of task generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n    \\item Single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), revealing a scale-dependent layer interaction: the semantic layer alone achieves 84\\% DR reduction at 70B vs.\\ 24\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}). Beam $k{=}2$ is Pareto-optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\\%, 1.18$\\times$ latency); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 1.39$\\times$.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,
3	%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema
4	
5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, $+$10\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\,$\to$\,0\%, 1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 41--47\%).
8	
9	\paragraph{Setup.}
10	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
11	RE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.
12	All constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.
13	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
14	LoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.
15	Our R-F1 range (41--47\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\%~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.
```

95	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
96	\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}
97	Under greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 245 cross-field violations on fine-tuned SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), of which 63.3\% (155/245) involve \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\%) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{Material} (12.3\%).
98	This reflects the architectural limitation of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, the first-seen type registration (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.
99	Beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all CF violations by exploring alternative type assignments, but at 1.54$\times$ latency (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
100	
101	\begin{table}[t]
102	\centering
103	\small
104	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
```

    "originalFile": "% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)\n% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,\n%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema\n\n\\label{sec:violation_landscape}\n\nWe evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: greedy S1 (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, $+$10\\% latency) and beam S1 (all violations\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, 1.54$\\times$ latency); zero-shot evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}) serves as a diagnostic probe for the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 41--47\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.}\nWe evaluate on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$).\nRE type constraints are extracted from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) derive from the KAIROS ontology.\nAll constraints are schema-level, independent of evaluation splits.\nPrimary model: Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}, composability with fine-tuning in \\S\\ref{sec:composability}.\nLoRA hyperparameters (rank, $\\alpha$, learning rate) were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization; given the substantial schema complexity difference (7 vs.\\ 96 relations), this transfer may underestimate DocRED fine-tuning potential.\nOur R-F1 range (41--47\\% under LoRA) is consistent with recent LLM-based generative RE evaluations on these benchmarks, where document-level closed IE without gold entity spans typically achieves 35--50\\%~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}; supervised span-based methods operating under different assumptions (gold entity spans, dedicated architectures) are not directly comparable.\nDocRED development documents were used for evaluation only.\nFour violation categories: \\textbf{DR} (type pair $\\notin \\sigma(r)$), \\textbf{RV} (relation $\\notin \\mathcal{R}$), \\textbf{ET} (entity type $\\notin \\mathcal{E}$), \\textbf{CF} (conflicting types across triples).\nBaseline DR: 15.37\\% (SciERC), 18.46\\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\\% but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\n",

78	This produces severe DR regressions under zero-shot (Llama: 2.55\%$\to$11.93\%; 70B: 0\%$\to$4.20\%), where baseline CF is high enough that many entities require resolution.
79	Under LoRA, baseline CF is already low (0.36--0.42\%), limiting both the scope for TCR improvement and the DR cost.
80	
81	This $\bar{\kappa}$-dependent boundary is consistent with the constraint tightness analysis in \S\ref{sec:composition}: $\bar{\kappa}$ characterizes not only the decoding-time DR residual regime but also the feasibility of post-hoc type resolution.
82	Beam search (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}) resolves CF and DR simultaneously across all $\bar{\kappa}$ values (SciERC and DocRED (998 docs): DR$\to$0\% for calibrated models), making it the preferred approach for high-tightness schemas.
83	TCR is a zero-cost complement for low-tightness schemas where beam search latency (1.54$\times$) is undesirable.
84	Integrating TCR into the decoding loop would require global optimization over all generated triples, conflicting with the autoregressive generation paradigm.
85	TCR is complementary to OntoCoDec---it addresses CF (which OntoCoDec's per-triple enforcement cannot prevent) while OntoCoDec addresses DR (which post-hoc correction cannot guarantee).
86

enforces domain/range constraints per-triple but does not enforce global entity type consensus, leaving residual CF violations where the same entity receives different types across triples.\nWe evaluate a post-hoc resolution step (TCR): for each entity with conflicting type assignments, TCR selects the type that maximizes satisfaction count across all relations referencing that entity, subject to domain/range constraints.\nUnlike naive majority voting (which ignores relational constraints and increases DR), TCR is constraint-aware: candidate types are scored by how many of the schemas).\nOn SciERC ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), fundamental conflicts constitute 0--2\\% of type disagreements: most conflicts are resolvable because the loose schema admits many valid type pairs, and TCR can find a type satisfying all relational contexts.\nDR increases are bounded ($\\leq$0.28\\,pp), and E-F1 cost is modest ($\\leq$1.78\\,pp in zero-shot, $\\leq$0.75\\,pp under LoRA).\nOn DocRED ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$), 13--23\\% of conflicts are fundamental: the tight schema renders most type pairs invalid, so unifying an entity's type across all its relational contexts inevitably violates some domain/range constraints.\nThis produces severe DR regressions under zero-shot (Llama: 2.55\\%$\\to$11.93\\%; 70B: 0\\%$\\to$4.20\\%), where baseline CF is high enough that many entities approach for high-tightness schemas.\nTCR is a zero-cost complement for low-tightness schemas where beam search latency (1.54$\\times$) is undesirable.\nIntegrating TCR into the decoding loop would require global optimization over all generated triples, conflicting with the autoregressive generation paradigm.\nTCR is complementary to OntoCoDec---it addresses CF (which OntoCoDec's per-triple enforcement cannot prevent) while OntoCoDec addresses DR (which post-hoc correction cannot guarantee).\n",

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

[Director 指令] [URGENT 修正] §5.1 CF 数据标注错误 + SF4 cascade 分析数据

## 数据修正（critical，必须在所有其他修改之前或同时处理）

**section5_scierc_draft.tex L96-99 当前段落有严重数据错误**：

"245 cross-field violations on fine-tuned SciERC" 是错的——245 是 **ZS** 的 unique_cf_entity_doc_pairs，不是 FT 的。

实际数据：
- **ZS** (phase2_scierc_full_s1): 248 CF violations, 245 unique entity-doc pairs, 167 docs
- **FT** (w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval): **22** CF violations, 21 unique pairs, 20 docs

"155/245 = 63.3% Generic↔X" 也来自 ZS 数据。FT Generic 参与仅 7/22。

**修改方案**（二选一，推荐方案 A）：

**方案 A（推荐）**：该段落叙事意图是分析 CF 成因——ZS 数据（n=248）比 FT（n=22）更有解释力。改为明确标注 ZS：

"Under zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\% involve \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\%) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{Material} (12.3\%). Fine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (\S\ref{sec:composability})."

**方案 B**：改为 FT 数据——但 n=22 样本量小，分解不如 ZS 有力。

## SF4 cascade 分析数据（直接回应 R24 审稿人）

Reviewer 独立分析得到的 cascade 统计——写入 §5 CF 段或 Appendix：

**ZS cascade breakdown (n=248)**:
- True cascade error（first-seen 锁错→阻止正确类型）: 7.3% (18/248)
- Hallucinated entity（模型能力问题，非约束机制）: 58.1% (144/248)
- Correct lock（first-seen 锁定正确）: 18.1%
- correct_lock > cascade_error 2.5×——first-seen 锁定净正面

**FT cascade**: cascade_error = **0/22**——fine-tuning 完全消除级联错误

**叙事要点**：
1. First-seen registration 是净正面机制（正确锁定 2.5× 多于级联错误）
2. ZS CF 主要来自幻觉实体（58.1%），不是约束机制缺陷
3. Fine-tuning 后级联错误完全消失——证明 first-seen 问题仅在 ZS（模型不够好时）暴露

这些数据直接回应 SF4"first-seen 级联未量化"的复发问题。建议在 §5 CF 段加 2-3 句，详细分解放 Appendix。

## 同步检查

在修改 CF 相关段落时，请同步检查：
1. Table 2 (tab:composability) 中 Qwen FT SciERC S1 的 CF 列是否与 22/N 一致（报告中 CF=1.18%）
2. Abstract 中关于 CF 的任何数字是否受影响
3. §5.1 composability 段（L86-91）中引用的 CF 数字是否一致

77	On DocRED (96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$), grammar-only \emph{increases} DR via \textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing OOV$\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).

79	DR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
80	Zero-shot evaluation thus establishes violation prevalence and constraint interaction; fine-tuning demonstrates practical elimination.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NnSWkv51pQRMgqZfHGckkm`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.
3	These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).
4	
5	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
6	A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
7	
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning near-eliminates domain/range violations ($<$\,0.1\%) at F1 41--47\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\leq$1.4\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.
10	Beam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\times$.
11	In zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\%) but decrease with model calibration.
12	Zero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.
13	\end{abstract}
14	
```

**Current §5.1 CF paragraph (L96-99)**:
```
\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}
Under greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 245 cross-field violations on fine-tuned SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), of which 63.3\% (155/245) involve \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\%) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{Material} (12.3\%).


```

The root cause is that the per-triple enforcement mechanism doesn't coordinate across multiple references to the same entity—the first type assignment gets locked in and conflicts with later requirements. Beam search with k=4 resolves this by considering alternative type assignments, though it comes with a 1.39× latency cost.

I need to fix the attribution errors: the 245 violations and 63.3% ratio both come from zero-shot data, not fine-tuned. The composability section at line 91 mentions 63.3% for Generic↔X ambiguity without explicitly marking it as zero-shot, so I should verify whether that percentage applies to the overall distribution or just the zero-shot case.

```
Qwen3-8B & SciERC & Unconstr. & 1.95
```

Checking Table 2 for composability metrics...

The Qwen FT SciERC S1 row shows CF=1.18%, which aligns with 22 violations out of roughly 1,864 total predictions. The table data checks out.

Now I need to work on the cascade analysis for section 5 — Director wants 2-3 sentences covering the SF4 findings.

Looking at the key numbers: zero-shot has a true cascade error rate of 7.3% (18 out of 248 cases), with hallucinated entities accounting for 58.1% of those violations. The correct lock rate sits at 18.1%, which is 2.5 times higher than the cascade error rate. Fine-tuned models show zero cascade errors across the 22 violations.

My plan is to rewrite the CF paragraph in section 5.1 using the Director's approach, integrate the cascade analysis findings, double-check the 63.3% reference for consistency with the zero-shot analysis, and prepare the appendix with detailed cascade breakdowns. Then I'll draft the new CF paragraph that incorporates both the cross-field violation context and the cascade error insights. Looking at the cascade analysis results, first-seen registration actually works well overall since correct type locks happen much more frequently than true cascade errors. After fine-tuning, the violation count drops dramatically to just 22 across 20 documents, and cascade errors disappear entirely. Beam search with k=4 solves all violations by exploring alternative type assignments, though it increases latency by 1.39×.

For the composability section, I'm noting that the 63.3% concentration in Generic↔X ambiguity comes from the zero-shot distribution, which is more statistically reliable given fine-tuning only has 22 violations to work with.
````

</details>

230	
231	%%% CF Violation Distribution %%%
232	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (CF=245), llama_scierc_full_s1 (CF=162),
233	%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (CF=2670), llama_docred_full_s1 (CF=3824)
234	
235	\paragraph{Cross-Field Violation Distribution.}
236	\label{app:cf-distribution}
237	
238	Table~\ref{tab:cf-distribution} reports the complete distribution of conflicting-type pairs for SciERC S1 (245 CF violations, all pairwise; no mention triggered $\geq$3 types).
239	Conflicts involving the \textsc{Generic} type account for 63.3\% (155/245) of all violations.
240	\textsc{Generic} is SciERC's anaphoric/placeholder label (``this approach'', ``the method''), which competes with every content type depending on whether the mention is used anaphorically or referentially, a distinction the per-slot constrained decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.
241	
242	The pattern generalizes across models and datasets: on Llama-3.1-8B SciERC, \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} remains the top pair (53/162, 32.7\%); on DocRED, the analogous catch-all type \textsc{MISC} drives 76.6\% of Qwen's CF violations.
243	Under unconstrained decoding, CF rates collapse to $\leq$1\% on both datasets; CF violations are a side-effect of per-slot type commitment rather than a model deficiency.
244	
245	\begin{table}[t]
246	\centering
247	\small
248	\begin{tabular}{@{}lr r@{}}
249	\toprule
250	\textbf{Conflicting Type Pair} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{\%} \\
251	\midrule
252	Generic $\leftrightarrow$ OtherScientificTerm & 85 & 34.7 \\
253	Generic $\leftrightarrow$ Material & 35 & 14.3 \\
254	Generic $\leftrightarrow$ Metric & 30 & 12.2 \\
255	Method $\leftrightarrow$ Task & 20 & 8.2 \\
256	Material $\leftrightarrow$ Method & 17 & 6.9 \\
257	OtherScientificTerm $\leftrightarrow$ Task & 12 & 4.9 \\
258	\midrule
259	10 further pairs (each $\leq$3.3\%) & 46 & 18.8 \\
260	\midrule
261	\textbf{Total} & \textbf{245} & 100.0 \\
262	\bottomrule
263	\end{tabular}
264	\caption{CF violation distribution on SciERC S1 (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents).
265	All 245 violations are pairwise conflicts.
266	Conflicts involving \textsc{Generic} account for 63.3\%.}
267	\label{tab:cf-distribution}
268	\end{table}
269	
270	%%% Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning %%%
271	% exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1
272	
273	\paragraph{Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning.}
274	\label{app:lora-per-relation}
275	
276	Table~\ref{tab:lora-per-relation} presents the per-relation breakdown of DR elimination when OntoCoDec is composed with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC.
277	Fine-tuning alone eliminates DR on four of seven relations but leaves non-zero violations on the three tightest-constraint relations: \textsc{Evaluate-for} ($\kappa{=}0.361$, 13.13\%), \textsc{Part-of} ($\kappa{=}0.417$, 5.77\%), and \textsc{Compare} ($\kappa{=}0.528$, 5.00\%).
278	Adding OntoCoDec constraints eliminates all remaining violations across all relations.
279	One \textsc{Evaluate-for} triple (99\,$\to$\,98) is redirected to \textsc{Used-for} (634\,$\to$\,635) by token-level masking: the head entity's registered type falls outside \textsc{Evaluate-for}'s domain, and the semantic mask steers generation to the nearest valid relation.

\\emph{increases} DR via \\textit{latent violation surfacing}: forcing OOV$\\to$valid relation mapping exposes violations previously masked by vocabulary errors (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-zs}).\nCross-model validation (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}) confirms DR drops of 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED, with R-F1 improving in 5/6 settings; post-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but at 15--74\\% rejection rates.\nDR-to-CF conversion represents a severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream-impact}).\nUnder fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\\Delta$DR$<$0.2\\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--69\\% reduction; Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\\kappa \\geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).\nFallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\\% for Llama, confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  CF quantification (MF1 response)\n% {
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\caption{CF violation distribution on SciERC S1 (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents).\nAll 245 violations are pairwise conflicts.\nConflicts involving \\textsc{Generic} account for 63.3\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cf-distribution}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning %%%",
  "new_string": "\\caption{CF violation distribution on SciERC zero-shot S1 (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents).\nAll 245 unique entity-document pairs involve pairwise conflicts.\nConflicts involving \\textsc{Generic} account for 63.3\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cf-distribution}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Cascade Error Analysis %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22)\n\n\\paragraph{Cascade Error Analysis.}\n\\label{app:cascade-analysis}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cascade-breakdown} decomposes zero-shot CF violations by source mechanism.\nThe dominant source is hallucinated entities (58.1\\%): the model generates entity names absent from the input document, and the entity registry assigns a type based on the first occurrence that may conflict with a subsequent valid entity sharing the same surface form.\nTrue cascade errors---where first-seen type registration for a \\emph{real} entity locks an incorrect type that blocks a subsequent valid assignment---account for only 7.3\\% (18/248).\nCorrect type locks (first-seen type is correct and prevents an incorrect subsequent assignment) are 2.5$\\times$ more frequent (18.1\\%, 45/248), indicating that the first-seen registration mechanism provides a net benefit for type consistency.\n\nUnder fine-tuning (22 CF violations, 20 documents), cascade errors are completely eliminated (0/22): LoRA training calibrates entity typing sufficiently that first-seen registrations are consistently correct.\nThis confirms that cascade errors are a model calibration issue exposed by the constraint mechanism, not an inherent deficiency of first-seen registration.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{CF Source Category} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nHallucinated entity & 144 & 58.1 \\\\\nCorrect type lock & 45 & 18.1 \\\\\nTrue cascade error & 18 & 7.3 \\\\\nOther / ambiguous & 41 & 16.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Total} & \\textbf{248} & 100.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{CF violation source breakdown on SciERC zero-shot S1 (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents; exp\\_id: phase2\\_scierc\\_full\\_s1).\n``Hallucinated entity'': generated entity absent from input.\n``True cascade error'': first-seen lock on incorrect type blocks valid subsequent assignment.\n``Correct type lock'': first-seen type correct, prevents incorrect subsequent type.\nUnder fine-tuning, cascade errors are completely eliminated (0/22 CF violations; exp\\_id: w5\\_lora\\_scierc\\_full\\_s1\\_eval).}\n\\label{tab:cascade-breakdown}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning %%%"
}

88	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% with no significant $\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\% CIs $\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream-impact}).
89	Under fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\Delta$DR$<$0.2\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--69\% reduction; Appendix~\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\kappa \geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).
90	Fallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\% for Llama, confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.
91	CF drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\% of zero-shot CF; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
92	
93	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
94	%  CF quantification (MF1 response)
95	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
96	\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}
97	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248, 167 docs), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22, 20 docs)
98	Under zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\% involve \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\%) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{Material} (12.3\%).
99	A source breakdown (Appendix~\ref{app:cascade-analysis}) shows 58.1\% originate from hallucinated entities (a model capacity issue), while true cascade errors---where first-seen registration locks an incorrect type---account for only 7.3\%; correct locks are 2.5$\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.
100	Fine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).
101	Beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\times$ latency (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
102

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep
11	With LoRA, beam search~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED at both 8B and 70B scales (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
12	A beam-width sweep ($k \in \{1,2,4,8\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\%, at only 1.18$\times$ greedy latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\times$ latency (Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
13	This yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\%, CF${\leq}$1.4\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\times$, all violations${\to}$0\%).
14	Per-beam memory overhead is $O(k \times |\text{entities}| \times |\text{types}|)$ for independent entity registry copies, negligible at typical document scales.
15	
16	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
17	\paragraph{70B-class validation.}
18	\label{sec:70b-results}
19	The following results constitute a single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4); cross-family verification (e.g., Qwen-72B) remains future work.
20	All 70B experiments use Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct under AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms constraint enforcement is unaffected by quantization (Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}).
21	Zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\%, DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\%; R-F1 11--13).
22	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)
23	A four-way ablation reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\% DR reduction vs.\ 8B's 24\% (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), though grammar remains necessary for RV elimination and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).
24	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained
25	LoRA confirms composability: DR\,$\to$\,0\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\Delta| \leq 0.20$\,pp; CF 0.18--1.33\%); cross-family generalization at 70B scale remains to be verified.
26	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_docred_200_beam4_s1
27	Beam ($k{=}4$) reduces DocRED parse failures (12.5\%\,$\to$\,0.5\%, R-F1 $+$4.19\,pp); SciERC controlled comparison isolates pure beam gain at $+$0.28\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:70b-ablation}).
28	
29	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
30	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
31	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
32	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \texttt{guided\_json}~\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence on Qwen3-8B (DR${=}$15.26\%; other models use the same backend; Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
33	The ${\sim}$15\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\% total reduction); trie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\% parse failure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}).
34	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EiDdYysMGZSWorGEh818sB`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.
3	These violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).
4	
5	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
6	A decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
7	
8	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
9	Evaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning near-eliminates domain/range violations ($<$\,0.1\%) at F1 41--47\%, but introduces cross-field type inconsistencies ($\leq$1.4\%) as the dominant residual---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.
10	Beam search ($k{=}2$) reduces CF to 0.11\% at 1.18$\times$ latency; $k{=}4$ eliminates all violation types at 1.39$\times$.
11	In zero-shot settings, cross-field rates are higher (up to 12\%) but decrease with model calibration.
12	Zero-shot domain/range reductions span 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; violation rates follow an observed pattern consistent with schema restrictiveness across our three evaluation schemas ($n{=}3$; insufficient for statistical inference), with a scale-dependent relationship between the two layers.
13	\end{abstract}
14	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LeJXXbJDLGm5pLbefs3PP4`</summary>

```text
88	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% with no significant $\Delta$R-F1 (Bonferroni-corrected; 95\% CIs $\subseteq [-0.69, {+}0.25]$\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream-impact}).
89	Under fine-tuning, grammar becomes a no-op ($\Delta$DR$<$0.2\,pp), making the semantic layer the sole DR defense (39--69\% reduction; Appendix~\ref{app:ft-ablation}); three relations with $\kappa \geq 0.36$ retain DR, all eliminated by S1 (Appendix~\ref{app:lora-per-relation}).
90	Fallback frequency is model-dependent: Qwen triggers 0 fallbacks under LoRA + S1 on SciERC, while Llama triggers 5{,}882 (Table~\ref{tab:ft-ablation}), reflecting weaker entity typing; despite this, DR reaches 0.09\% for Llama, confirming that even frequent fallback does not compromise enforcement.
91	CF drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity (63.3\% of zero-shot CF; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types (R-F1 43--47; \S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
92	
93	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
94	%  CF quantification (MF1 response)
95	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
96	\paragraph{Cross-field violation analysis.}
97	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (ZS: CF=248, 167 docs), w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (FT: CF=22, 20 docs)
98	Under zero-shot greedy S1, CF is the sole residual violation type (DR, RV, ET all eliminated): 248 cross-field violations arise across 167 documents, of which 63.3\% involve \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X type ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}), concentrated in \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} (34.7\%) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{Material} (12.3\%).
99	A source breakdown (Appendix~\ref{app:cascade-analysis}) shows 58.1\% originate from hallucinated entities (a model capacity issue), while true cascade errors---where first-seen registration locks an incorrect type---account for only 7.3\%; correct locks are 2.5$\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.
100	Fine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).
101	Beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\times$ latency (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
102	
103	\begin{table}[t]

correct locks are 2.5$\\times$ more frequent, confirming first-seen registration as a net-positive mechanism.\nFine-tuning reduces CF to 22 violations across 20 documents (1.18\\%), with cascade errors completely eliminated (0/22).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all remaining CF at 1.39$\\times$ latency (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AQYt63ByjLkbMZe6Gi751w`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.
4	Because IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.
5	This scale-dependent interaction (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.
6	
7	\paragraph{DR$\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}
8	Greedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\,pp).
9	This is an architectural trade-off of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, first-seen type registration (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.
10	Beam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\% at only 1.18$\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\times$ (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
11	The trade-off represents a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}
14	GoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance.
15	OntoCoDec provides a runtime guarantee layer---the two approaches are complementary rather than competing.
16	LoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\%, and constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.
17	
18	\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\ global enforcement.}
19	The fundamental tension is that per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations, which correspond to Level~3 (non-local) constraints in our taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:decidability}).
20	Global enforcement requires maintaining entity-level type consensus across the full output sequence, exceeding what online per-token masking can achieve.
21	Beam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.
22	Constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\times$ compression; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.
23	
```

    "oldString": "Beam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.\nConstraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.",
    "newString": "Beam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.",
    "originalFile": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nOntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.\nBecause IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\\% vs.\\ 94\\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.\nThis scale-dependent interaction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.\nConstraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp).\nThis is an architectural trade-off of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, first-seen type registration (\\S\\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.\nBeam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at only 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nThe trade-off represents a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance.\nOntoCoDec provides a runtime guarantee layer---the two approaches are complementary rather than competing.\nLoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\\%, and constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\\ global enforcement.}\nThe fundamental tension is that per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations, which correspond to Level~3 (non-local) constraints in our taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}).\nGlobal enforcement requires maintaining entity-level type consensus across the full output sequence, exceeding what online per-token masking can achieve.\nBeam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.\nConstraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\\times$ compression; Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.\n",

    "originalFile": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\n% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep\nWith LoRA, beam search~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on both SciERC and DocRED at both 8B and 70B scales (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nA beam-width sweep ($k \\in \\{1,2,4,8\\}$) on SciERC reveals $k{=}2$ as Pareto-optimal: R-F1 44.60 (highest), CF 0.11\\%, at only 1.18$\\times$ greedy latency; $k{=}4$ achieves CF${=}$0\\% but lower R-F1 (43.60) at 1.39$\\times$ latency (Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\nThis yields three deployment tiers: greedy S1 ($+$10\\% latency, DR${<}$0.1\\%, CF${\\leq}$1.4\\%), beam $k{=}2$ (1.18$\\times$, near-zero CF), and beam $k{=}4$ (1.39$\\times$, all violations${\\to}$0\\%).\nPer-beam memory overhead is $O(k \\times |\\text{entities}| \\times |\\text{types}|)$ for independent entity registry copies, negligible at typical document scales.\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B-class validation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nThe following results constitute a single-family scale validation on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4); cross-family verification (e.g., Qwen-72B) remains future work.\nAll 70B experiments use Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct under AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms constraint enforcement is unaffected by quantization (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\nZero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets (SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%, DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; R-F1 11--13).\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_ablation (grammar_only, semantic_only, full_s1)\nA four-way ablation reveals scale-dependent layer interaction: semantic-only achieves 84\\% DR reduction vs.\\ 8B's 24\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}), though grammar remains necessary for RV elimination and CF control (semantic-only CF${=}$76).\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained\nLoRA confirms composability: DR\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% on both datasets (R-F1 44.53, 43.95; $|\\Delta| \\leq 0.20$\\,pp; CF 0.18--1.33\\%); cross-family generalization at 70B scale remains to be verified.\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_docred_200_beam4_s1\nBeam ($k{=}4$) reduces DocRED parse failures (12.5\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.5\\%, R-F1 $+$4.19\\,pp); SciERC controlled comparison isolates pure beam gain at $+$0.28\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}; vLLM \\texttt{guided\\_json}~\\cite{kwon2023vllm} confirms equivalence on Qwen3-8B (DR${=}$15.26\\%; other models use the same backend; Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nThe ${\\sim}$15\\% residual DR is the gap our semantic layer addresses (94\\% total reduction); trie-only constraints without JSON enforcement yield 9--51\\% parse failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}).\n",

```text
1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.
4	Because IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.
5	This scale-dependent interaction (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.
6	Constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\times$ compression; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.
7	
8	\paragraph{DR$\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}
9	Greedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\,pp).
10	This is an architectural trade-off of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, first-seen type registration (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.
11	Beam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\% at only 1.18$\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\times$ (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
12	The trade-off represents a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
13	
14	\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}
15	GoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance.
16	OntoCoDec provides a runtime guarantee layer---the two approaches are complementary rather than competing.
17	LoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\%, and constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.
18	
19	\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\ global enforcement.}
20	The fundamental tension is that per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations, which correspond to Level~3 (non-local) constraints in our taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:decidability}).
21	Global enforcement requires maintaining entity-level type consensus across the full output sequence, exceeding what online per-token masking can achieve.
22	Beam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.
23	
```

</details>

[Director 指令] [URGENT] 页面溢出修复 + Reviewer 5-item 残余项

## 最高优先级：§7 溢出到第 9 页

EMNLP 2026 严格 8 页限制。必须压缩 ~8 行让 §7 结束在第 8 页内。

压缩策略（按优先级）：
1. §7 Discussion 三段中找冗余——每段压缩 2-3 行即可（去重复论证、合并短句）
2. §6 Related Work 进一步精简（如果还有空间）
3. §5 散布段落微压缩（去 filler words）
4. **不要删除 substantive content**——只压缩表述

## Reviewer 残余项（如果之前未处理）

- **Warning #3**：§5.3 70B 标题 `\paragraph{70B-class validation.}` → `\paragraph{70B-class validation (single-family).}`
- **Warning #5**：§7 "Constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization..." 句从 per-triple vs global 段移到 §7 第一段或 §5.3 末尾

## 完成后

编译确认 ≤ 8 页正文，然后提交 Reviewer 做最终验证。
