```
% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion

OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.
Because IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.


```

Now I'm looking at the trade-offs between decoding rate and constraint feasibility. As models scale up, grammar-constrained decoding alone becomes less effective, but the approach remains robust under quantization for practical deployment. The greedy strategy minimizes decoding rate violations but introduces constraint feasibility issues—this is the fundamental tension when enforcing constraints independently per triple.

When multiple triples share entities, the first-seen type registration can conflict with later type requirements. Beam search offers a better balance: with k=2, I can reduce constraint feasibility issues to near-zero while keeping latency overhead minimal, and k=4 eliminates them entirely at modest cost. The key insight is that decoding rate violations are permanent without re-extraction, whereas constraint feasibility issues—valid triples with inconsistent types across the output—can be fixed afterward through post-hoc resolution techniques.

```
Training-based approaches like GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge during learning but can't enforce it at runtime. OntoCoDec adds a runtime guarantee layer, making them complementary rather than competing. The combination works: training reduces decoding rate violations to 1-2%, and constraints handle what remains, achieving both high quality and near-zero violations.

The core challenge is that enforcing constraints per-triple can't coordinate type assignments across the full output—that's where constraint feasibility violations come from. Global enforcement needs entity-level type consensus maintained throughout decoding, which per-token masking alone can't provide. Beam search approximates this, post-hoc correction handles residual issues, but the cleanest solution would integrate entity-level type tracking directly into the decoding loop.
``` I'm working through a compression strategy to fit the overflow. The key is to merge sentences in section 7 — combining the summary opening, tightening the discussion of why domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, and consolidating the complementarity and per-triple paragraphs. I estimate this saves around 8 printed lines if I'm aggressive enough, which gives me breathing room on the page.

Now I'm looking at the compressed version of section 7 itself. The opening merges two sentences with a semicolon to describe how OntoCoDec validates across datasets and models. The next part combines the explanation of why semantic enforcement matters — the 3% versus 94% reduction gap shows grammar alone isn't enough — with the forward masking mechanism and quantization robustness. For the trade-off paragraph, I need to finish the thought about how greedy S1 reduces deletion rate but introduces constraint violations, and how the entity registry's first-seen registration creates this architectural tension.

Then beam search emerges as the mitigation: k=2 hits a Pareto point with minimal violations at modest latency cost, while k=4 eliminates violations entirely. The key insight is that deletion rate is permanent damage while constraint violations can be fixed post-hoc with the TCR method. Finally, the complementarity section contrasts training-based approaches like GoLLIE and KnowCoder — which internalize schema but can't guarantee runtime compliance — with OntoCoDec's runtime guarantee layer. I've merged those sections too, which should free up another couple of lines. That brings me to roughly seven or eight lines of savings overall—enough to make room for what I need to add.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KZmTgqdaWH8cHFzswru8wc`</summary>

```text
1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints, validated across 3 datasets, 3+ models, and 2 tasks.
4	Because IE type constraints exceed context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), the semantic layer is necessary to bridge the gap grammar-only enforcement cannot address (3\% vs.\ 94\% DR reduction at 8B), using constructive forward masking over a dynamic entity registry.
5	This scale-dependent interaction (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}) suggests grammar-constrained decoding is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.
6	Constraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (4$\times$ compression; Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), confirming deployment viability.
7	
8	\paragraph{DR$\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}
9	Greedy S1 reduces DR to ${<}$0.1\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\,pp).
10	This is an architectural trade-off of per-triple independent enforcement: when multiple triples reference the same entity, first-seen type registration (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) may conflict with subsequent type requirements.
11	Beam search mitigates this: $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\% at only 1.18$\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal); $k{=}4$ eliminates all CF at 1.39$\times$ (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
12	The trade-off represents a net severity downgrade: DR is irrecoverable without re-extraction, while CF---individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency---is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR eliminates 18--92\%; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
13	
14	\paragraph{Complementarity with training-based enforcement.}
15	GoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but cannot guarantee runtime compliance.
16	OntoCoDec provides a runtime guarantee layer---the two approaches are complementary rather than competing.
17	LoRA + S1 validates this: training reduces DR to 1--2\%, and constraints eliminate the residual; neither alone achieves both high R-F1 and near-zero DR.
18	
19	\paragraph{Per-triple vs.\ global enforcement.}
20	The fundamental tension is that per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments---the source of CF violations, which correspond to Level~3 (non-local) constraints in our taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:decidability}).
21	Global enforcement requires maintaining entity-level type consensus across the full output sequence, exceeding what online per-token masking can achieve.
22	Beam search approximates this; TCR provides post-hoc correction; the most direct path is integrating entity-level type consensus into the decoding loop, which we leave to future work.
23	
```

</details>

\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}).\nGlobal enforcement requires entity-level type consensus across the full output, exceeding online per-token masking; beam search approximates this, TCR provides post-hoc correction, and integrating entity-level consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n"
}
```
